20 > 18, we win.
But wait, "The winner also takes losses (attrition against the loser's effective force)."
Loser's effective force is the defender's effective defense, which is 18.
How many troops do we lose?
Usually, the winner loses some troops. Let's assume we lose some archers or knights.
But we should still win and capture the village.
Is there any risk that the enemy reinforces Teruel?
The enemy writes their orders at the same time as us, over the same snapshot.
So they don't know we are attacking Teruel until we attack.
But they might have troops in Zaragoza [17] or Castellon [18] that they send to Teruel.
However, "An army STOPS at the first village on its path that is not yours and fights there, even if you ordered a more distant destination."
If they send from Zaragoza [17] to Teruel [13], they arrive at Teruel. But since we attack on turn 16, and their march from Zaragoza to Teruel takes some turns, they might arrive on turn 16 or later.
But we attack first? "Orders are SIMULTANEOUS".
If they send from Zaragoza to Teruel, and we send from Toledo to Teruel, both arrive on turn 16.
But "Troops sent from DIFFERENT villages never add up, even when they arrive at the same target on the same turn: each send fights alone, one at a time."
So our send from Toledo fights the defender in Teruel. If the defender is still there, we fight them. If the enemy send from Zaragoza arrives, they fight the winner of the first battle? Or do they fight separately?
"each send fights alone, one at a time."
This means the garrison fights first, then the first reinforcement, then the second reinforcement.
If we attack with Toledo, we fight the garrison (1 spearman, 4 archers, 2 knights). We win, we take the village.
Then the enemy send from Zaragoza arrives and fights us (the new garrison of Teruel, which is our survivors).
So we might lose the village if the enemy reinforcement is strong.
Zaragoza has 7 spearmen, 6 archers, 5 knights. If they send all, it's very strong.
But would they send all? They might not know we are attacking.
Also, Zaragoza is their capital's defender, they might want to keep it strong.
But to be safe, we should attack Teruel.
Wait, is there a way to prevent them from reinforcing?

Madrid [12] borders Zaragoza [17]. Madrid has 5 archers. Attack: 10. Defense 45 -> we lose. So we can't attack Zaragoza.

Another one: "King A attacked YOUR [13] Teruel with 2 spearmen, 2 archers, 4 knights (effective force 22 vs your defense 15): the village FELL. Your garrison (1 spearman, 2 archer, 3 knights) was destroyed."
Here, the loser was the defender (us, defense 15). The attacker won.
"Our garrison was destroyed" - because the village fell, the entire garrison is destroyed?
"The LOSER is destroyed entirely - this includes an attacker that fails against a village."
Yes, if the attacker fails, their entire army is destroyed. If the defender loses, their entire garrison is destroyed!
Wait, "The WINNER loses troops worth HALF of the loser's effective force, spread over its troop types"
So if the defender loses, their entire garrison is destroyed. The attacker loses troops worth half of the defender's effective force (which is the defender's defense, 15).
Half of 15 is 7.5. So the attacker loses troops worth 7.5.
If we attack Madrid (def 10), the defender's entire garrison (2 spearman, 1 archer, 1 knight) will be destroyed, and we will lose troops worth half of 10 = 5.
How is "worth 5" calculated? "spread over its troop types".
Our army has spearman, archer, knight. We will lose some of them.
Since we want to preserve our knights (which are expensive and powerful), we should try to have our army consist of cheaper troops if we expect to lose some, or just accept the loss.
Actually, the loss is spread over the troop types we have, proportional to their presence or just randomly?
"spread over its troop types: that amount does not depend on the winner's size, so a bigger winning army loses a smaller share of itself."
It means the absolute value of troops lost is constant (half of loser's effective force), so if our army is larger, we lose a smaller percentage of it.
So sending a larger army is good because we conquer the village and keep a larger percentage of our troops!
But we also need to defend other places.

We want to send an army from Zaragoza [17] to Madrid [12].
Zaragoza has: 7 spearman, 4 archer, 5 knight.
Let's send: 4 archer, 3 knight, 1 spearman.
Total troops: 8.
Attack: 4*2 + 3*4 + 1*1 = 21.
Most numerous: archer (4). Defender's most numerous: spearman (2). Counter applies!
Effective attack: 21 * 1.5 = 31.5.
Defender's defense: 10.
We win easily.
Losses: half of defender's effective force (10) = 5.
So we lose troops worth 5.
Since we have spearman (1), archer (4), knight (3), the loss of 5 will be spread. Probably we lose some archers and knights, or spearman.
Let's see if we can send a slightly different composition to minimize loss of knights, but since the loss is spread, it might take some of each.
Wait, is it better to send more troops to make the loss a smaller percentage?
If we send all 16 troops: 7 spearman, 4 archer, 5 knight.
Most numerous: spearman (7). Defender's most numerous: spearman (2). No counter.
Attack: 7*1 + 4*2 + 5*4 = 35.
Defense: 10.
Losses: half of 10 = 5.
Since we sent 16 troops, losing 5 is only ~31% of our army.
If we send 8 troops, losing 5 is ~62% of our army.
So sending more troops is better in terms of percentage of troops lost, and it also ensures we have a larger garrison in Madrid after capture!
Wait, when we capture a village, the surviving attackers stay there as its new garrison.
"The surviving attackers stay there as its new garrison."
So if we send 16 troops, lose 5, we have 11 troops left to garrison Madrid.
If we send 8 troops, lose 5, we have 3 troops left to garrison Madrid.
Madrid is a border village, so we want a decent garrison there to defend it or launch further attacks.
But if we send all 16 troops from Zaragoza, Zaragoza will be empty!

Wait, the enemy's turn is simultaneous with ours.
"Orders are SIMULTANEOUS: the enemy writes their orders at the same time as you, over the same snapshot of the map you are reading now. Nothing you order this turn is visible to them before it happens."
So the enemy does NOT know we are attacking Madrid. They write their orders based on the current map where Madrid is theirs and Zaragoza is ours.
So they might order an attack on Zaragoza from Madrid or Teruel.
If they attack Zaragoza from Madrid, and we also attack Madrid from Zaragoza:
What happens?
"An army STOPS at the first village on its path that is not yours and fights there, even if you ordered a more distant destination."
If the enemy marches from Madrid to Zaragoza, they will arrive at Zaragoza and fight our garrison there.
If we send all troops from Zaragoza to Madrid, Zaragoza has 0 troops.

"troops at home: 16 / 300"
If we send all 16, Zaragoza has 0 troops.

Yes, a village with 0 troops has no defense except the location bonus, but with no troops to fight, the attacker wins automatically and occupies it.

- Valencia [16]: wood 70, iron 50. Can build: spearman, archer, knight.
- Murcia [15]: wood 40, iron 20. Can build: archer (20 wood, 10 iron).
- Pamplona [19]: wood 40, iron 20. Can build: archer (20 wood, 10 iron).

Let's think about the strategy.
I have 17 villages. I need 18 to win (if held for 2 turns).
Which village is the most vulnerable?
Huesca [20] has defense 13, but it's small. However, it's far from my main forces, and Zaragoza [17] is nearby.
Castellon [18] has defense 33.
Madrid [12] has defense 35.
Zaragoza [17] has defense 55.
Let's look at the connections to Madrid [12]:
- Salamanca [7] (mine)
- Toledo [8] (mine)
- Burgos [14] (mine)
- Zaragoza [17] (enemy)
I can attack Madrid from Salamanca, Toledo, or Burgos.
Salamanca has 2 archers available.
Toledo has 4 archers available.
Burgos has 2 spearman, 3 archer, 1 knight available.
If I attack Madrid from Burgos: 2 spearman, 3 archer, 1 knight.
Let's calculate the attack power:
Most numerous: spearman (2) or archer (3)? Archers are 3, spearman 2, knight 1. So most numerous is archer.
Archer vs Madrid's garrison (4 spearman, 5 archer, 5 knights). Most numerous is archer (5) or spearman (4) or knight (5). Tie between archer and knight. Tie breaks in order spearman, archer, knight. So archer is the most numerous? "ties in count break in the order spearman, archer, knight". If archer and knight tie, archer wins. So defender's most numerous is archer.
Archer vs Archer: no counter bonus.
Attack: 3 archers * 2 = 6. 2 spearmen * 1 = 2. 1 knight * 4 = 4. Total attack = 12.
Defense: Madrid effective defense is 35.
12 vs 35 is a huge loss. I need a much bigger army to attack Madrid.
To attack Madrid, I need to send a large army.
Can I send troops from Coimbra [3] to Salamanca [7] or Toledo [8]?
Coimbra has 3 spearman, 3 archer, 5 knight available.
From Coimbra to Salamanca: 2 slow / 1 medium / 1 fast turns.
From Coimbra to Toledo: 3 slow / 2 medium / 1 fast turns.
But wait, if I send them to Salamanca, they will arrive in 1 turn (fast), 1 turn (medium), 2 turns (slow).
Then from Salamanca to Madrid: 1 slow / 1 medium / 1 fast turns.
So total travel time from Coimbra to Madrid via Salamanca:
Knights: 1 (to Salamanca) + 1 (to Madrid) = 2 turns.
Archers: 1 + 1 = 2 turns.
Spearmen: 2 + 1 = 3 turns.
If I send them now, they arrive in 2 or 3 turns.
But wait, I can send them directly from Coimbra to Madrid?
Road network: Coimbra [3] is connected to Salamanca [7]. Salamanca [7] is connected to Madrid [12].
So I cannot send directly from Coimbra to Madrid; I must go through Salamanca.
Wait, "An army STOPS at the first village on its path that is not yours and fights there, even if you ordered a more distant destination."
Salamanca is mine, so they won't stop there, they will just pass through.
So I can order Coimbra -> Madrid. The path is Coimbra -> Salamanca -> Madrid.
Travel time: Coimbra to Salamanca is 2 slow / 1 medium / 1 fast. Salamanca to Madrid is 1 slow / 1 medium / 1 fast.
Total: 3 slow / 2 medium / 2 fast turns.
So spearmen take 3 turns, archers 2 turns, knights 2 turns.
If I send 3 spearman, 3 archer, 5 knight from Coimbra to Madrid:
Attack power:
Most numerous: knight (5). Knight vs Madrid's most numerous (archer/knight tie -> archer).
Knight vs Archer: Knight counters archer! Multiplier 1.5.
Attack: 5 knights * 4 * 1.5 = 30. 3 archers * 2 = 6. 3 spearmen * 1 = 3. Total attack = 39.
Defense: Madrid effective defense is 35.
39 vs 35. We win!
But wait, the troops from Coimbra are 3 spearman, 3 archer, 5 knight.
Let's check if Coimbra has that many available.
"AVAILABLE TO SEND NOW: 3 spearman, 3 archer, 5 knight (attack power if all sent: 29)"
Wait, the report says "attack power if all sent: 29". Why 29?
Let's calculate: 3 spearman (3) + 3 archer (6) + 5 knight (20) = 29. No counter bonus is included in the "attack power if all sent" because it depends on the defender.
If I send them to Madrid, the counter bonus applies, so it's 39.
But wait, Madrid has 35 defense. 39 > 35, so we win.
But we also need to consider the garrison. The garrison is 4 spearmen, 5 archers, 5 knights.
When we attack, the garrison fights. The effective defense 35 already includes the garrison and location bonus.
So 39 attack vs 35 defense should win.
But wait, what about the other villages?
Can I send troops from Toledo [8] to Madrid?
Toledo has 4 archers available.
Toledo to Madrid: 1 slow / 1 medium / 1 fast turns.
If I send 4 archers: attack 8. Too weak.
Can I send troops from Burgos [14] to Madrid?
Burgos has 2 spearman, 3 archer, 1 knight.
Burgos to Madrid: 1 slow / 1 medium / 1 fast turns.
Attack: 3 archers (most numerous). Archer vs Archer. Attack = 3*2 + 2*1 + 1*4 = 12. Too weak.

What about Zaragoza [17]?
Zaragoza is connected to Madrid [12], Teruel [13], Pamplona [19], Huesca [20], Tarragona [21].
I can attack Zaragoza from Teruel [13] or Pamplona [19].
Teruel has 41 troops: 2 spearman, 17 archer, 22 knight.
If I send them from Teruel to Zaragoza:
Teruel to Zaragoza: 1 slow / 1 medium / 1 fast turns.
Zaragoza defense is 55.
If I send all 41 troops:
Most numerous: knight (22). Knight vs Zaragoza's garrison (3 spearman, 6 archer, 13 knights). Most numerous is knight (13).
Knight vs Knight: no counter bonus.
Attack: 22 knights * 4 = 88. 17 archers * 2 = 34. 2 spearmen * 1 = 2. Total attack = 124.
Defense: 55.
124 vs 55. We win easily!
But wait, if I send all troops from Teruel, Teruel's defense becomes 0 (or just the remaining troops).
Teruel currently has 41 troops. If I send them all, Teruel is empty.
But Teruel is a border village with enemy on multiple sides (Zaragoza, Castellon).
If I empty Teruel, the enemy can attack it easily.
Also, I have reinforcements arriving at Teruel this turn:
- 1 spearman, 6 archers, 5 knights from Santarem (arrives in 1 turn)
- 7 archers, 4 knights from Evora (arrives in 1 turn)
So Teruel will have even more troops next turn.
But if I send all 41 troops to Zaragoza, they will arrive in 1 turn (fast), 1 turn (medium), 1 turn (slow). So they all arrive next turn.
Wait, travel time from Teruel to Zaragoza is 1 slow / 1 medium / 1 fast turns.
So they all arrive in 1 turn!
If they arrive next turn, they will fight Zaragoza.
But wait, if I send them, they leave Teruel now. So Teruel's garrison becomes 0.
Is that safe? The enemy might attack Teruel this turn. But the enemy doesn't have any armies sighted on the march.
"ENEMY (only what your watchmen can see): - none sighted"
So no enemy armies are currently marching towards Teruel.
But the enemy can send troops from Zaragoza or Castellon to Teruel.
If they send from Zaragoza, it takes 1 turn. If they send from Castellon, it takes 1 turn.
If I send my troops from Teruel to Zaragoza, they arrive next turn. If the enemy sends troops from Zaragoza to Teruel, they also arrive next turn.
Then next turn, my troops are in Zaragoza (fighting), and enemy troops are in Teruel (fighting the remaining garrison, which would be the reinforcements arriving from Santarem and Evora).
Wait, the reinforcements from Santarem and Evora are arriving at Teruel THIS turn (they were marching, arrive in 1 turn).
So next turn, Teruel will have:
- Reinforcements from
